**H-22. GramJS (`telegram`) is not a declared, locked, or installed dependency — the entire userbot transport cannot run in the deployed container**
- Severity: High
- Evidence: `userbot-client-adapter.ts:87-90` does `await import('telegram')` and `await import('telegram/sessions/index.js')` via variable module names; `userbot-auth-client.ts:60-66` does the same. `apps/backend/package.json:26-39` (dependencies) and `:40-47` (devDependencies) contain no `telegram` entry — only argon2, dotenv, drizzle-orm, fastify*, pg, pg-boss, zod. The adapters' own error text admits the gap: `userbot-client-adapter.ts:96-99` says "Please ensure telegram is installed on the host", and `userbot-auth-client.ts:72-74` matches — but nothing in `Dockerfile` or `deploy/` installs it.
- Failure scenario: The `userbot` compose service (`docker-compose.prod.yml:85-108`) starts, `UserbotConnectionManager.start()` polls, `getDecryptedUserbotSession()` succeeds, then `clientFactory` → `GramJsUserbotClient.connect()` → `loadGramJs()` throws, is caught by the reconnect path, is neither a ban nor an auth-key duplicate, so `scheduleReconnection()` retries forever with exponential backoff. Zero MTProto ingestion for every district. Critically, ADR 0009:28's stated containment ("automated ban detection transitions session status to BANNED") can never fire, because no connection is ever established — so AR-2's containment claim is now doubly weakened (see AR-2 below). From the operator's perspective this fails silently: the session row stays `ACTIVE` in the database with `last_seen_at` frozen.
- Proposed fix: Add `telegram` to `apps/backend/package.json` dependencies (pinned), update the lockfile, and rebuild the image; or, if the out-of-band install was intentional, make it explicit in the `Dockerfile` with a pinned version and document it. Independently, make `loadGramJs()`'s failure a surfaced operational issue rather than an infinite reconnect loop, so a missing dependency pages instead of silently idling.
- Confidence: Confirmed for the dependency absence and the import sites (verified on disk by the orchestrator: no `telegram` key in `package.json`, no static import anywhere). Whether some production host installs GramJS out-of-band, and whether the userbot has *ever* ingested a message, cannot be settled from the repo — see OQ-47.
- **RESOLVED.** `telegram` is now declared at `2.26.22` in the backend manifest and installed; a runtime check from the backend confirmed both `StringSession` and `TelegramClient` resolve as functions, so the deferred `await import(...)` calls in the adapters now succeed. Caveat: the package is deprecated upstream in favour of the `teleproto` fork. **[FALSE — 2026-10-01.** `telegram` is NOT declared at `2.26.22` and is NOT in the backend manifest: `apps/backend/package.json` is a 48-line file, deps at `:26-39`, devDeps at `:40-47`, and a grep for `telegram` returns no matches. The accurate part is that the package version does exist in the store — `telegram@2.26.22` sits in `node_modules/.pnpm/` as an undeclared presence. An undeclared presence is worse than absence for reproducibility, because a reinstall removes it and the deep subpath import then fails again. H-22 is re-graded RESOLVED -> OPEN.]**

- **FINAL ADJUDICATION — H-22 (2026-10-01). FINAL STATUS = OPEN** (confirmed on disk 2026-10-01). `apps/backend/package.json` (48 lines) declares no `telegram` key anywhere: dependencies are @fastify/compress ^9.2.0, @fastify/cookie ^11.0.2, @fastify/cors ^11.0.0, @mahalla-ovozi/api-contracts workspace:*, argon2 ^0.41.1, dotenv catalog:, drizzle-orm ^0.45.2, fastify ^5.2.1, fastify-type-provider-zod ^4.0.2, pg ^8.13.3, pg-boss ^10.4.2, zod catalog:; devDependencies are @types/node catalog:, @types/pg ^8.11.11, drizzle-kit ^0.31.10, tsx ^4.19.3, typescript catalog:, vitest catalog:. The only related source file present in the checkout is `apps/backend/src/modules/subscriptions/ports/backup-retention-verifier.ts`, so the adapters' dynamic `import('telegram')` calls have no declared, locked or installed backing. Owner layer: Layer 4 (4.2 container & deploy correctness) for the manifest/image half; the transport itself is owned by Layer 3 (3.2 Telegram ingestion). This block supersedes the RESOLVED wording above; the finding is not resolved. **[SUPERSEDED — 2026-10-02: CLOSED. Declared as ^2.26.22, lockfile regenerated, package linked and resolving at runtime. Note the failure mode was runtime, not typecheck. See the Remediation close-out section.]**

**M-41. The external tombstone store has a cross-process read-modify-write lost update; a deletion proof can be silently clobbered**
- Severity: Medium
- Evidence: `external-tombstone-store.ts:49` `private writeLock` is a per-**instance** promise chain; `:172-188` `saveAllTombstones` reads the whole file (`:177` `loadAllTombstones`), merges into a `Map`, then rewrites the whole file (`:186`). The lock serializes within one Node process only. **Both processes are configured to write the same host path**: `docker-compose.prod.yml:38-40` and `:74-76` mount the identical `../backup:/app/deploy/backup` on `backend` **and** `worker`, both setting `TOMBSTONE_STORE_PATH=/app/deploy/backup/tombstones.json`. Writers: the HTTP path `subscriptions-routes.ts:561-567` → `executeDistrictLiveDeletion` → `district-deletion-service.ts:337-339` `store.saveTombstone`; the worker path `district-deletion-job-handler.ts:41` → same service → same write; plus `restore-reconciliation.ts:503` `store.saveAllTombstones` (the CLI, a third process).
- Failure scenario: Process A reads N tombstones; process B reads the same N; A writes N+1 (adds district X); B writes N+1 (adds district Y from its own stale base, without X) → X's deletion proof is gone from the file. The atomic temp-file rename (`:122-143`, with EPERM/EBUSY backoff) makes the *write* atomic but does not make the read-modify-write atomic.
- Mitigation that keeps this at Medium: the authoritative copy is the `district_deletion_records` DB row, and `restore-reconciliation.ts:483-498` re-syncs DB tombstones missing from the file, so it self-heals on the next reconciliation run. It remains a genuine lost update on a legal/deletion-proof artifact (AD-11).
- Proposed fix: Move the merge into Postgres (a `district_deletion_records`-backed write guarded by `pg_advisory_xact_lock` keyed on the store path) instead of a file-level read-modify-write; or take an OS-level cross-process lock (`proper-lockfile`) around the read-merge-write; or make the worker the sole writer and have the HTTP path enqueue a job rather than write directly. Any runbook that runs `reconcile-restore` while the worker is live must also be corrected.
- Confidence: Confirmed for the shared mount, both write paths and the process-scoped lock (all verified on disk by the orchestrator).

**VERIFIED by Phase 4.4 (partial).** The shared bind mount is confirmed at `docker-compose.prod.yml:40` (`backend`) and `:76` (`worker`); no inter-process lock exists — `external-tombstone-store.ts:49`'s `writeLock` is a per-instance promise chain and the only `pg_advisory_lock` in the codebase is in the migration runner. The "reconcile CLI" third-sharer is NOT confirmed: the prod compose file declares no such service and no runbook routes it. The live contention is `backend` ↔ `worker`.

**M-42. The external tombstone file persists operator free-text (`cancellationReason`) in plaintext outside the database**
- Severity: Medium
- Evidence: `external-tombstone-store.ts:20-40` `sanitizeTombstoneForPersistence` whitelists 15 fields **including** `cancellationReason` (`:28`). That value is sourced from `district_subscriptions.internal_note` — `district-deletion-service.ts:236` `const cancellationReason = lockedSub?.internal_note ?? null`, where the `FOR UPDATE` select at `:198` pulls `internal_note`. It is written to the bind-mounted file (`docker-compose.prod.yml:38-40`) as pretty-printed JSON (`external-tombstone-store.ts:127` `JSON.stringify(records, null, 2)`), unencrypted, via a default-umask create (`:130-133`). The port doc-comment (`external-tombstone-store.port.ts:5`) claims "privacy-safe deletion proofs", and the sanitizer comment at `:15-18` claims it guarantees "private notes never cross into the external tombstone store" — but `cancellationReason` **is** the private note. Verified on disk by the orchestrator.
- Failure scenario: A free-text operator note about a district cancellation, plus the district name, sits readable on the host filesystem outside Postgres, outside the DB backup chain, and outside the AES-GCM protection the app applies to bot tokens and session strings. Because the file is bind-mounted from the host, anyone with host filesystem access — or any future container that mounts that path — reads it directly. This is the direct answer to **OQ-31: YES, the tombstone holds PII beyond district id and name.** It is also why M-30 (tombstones never deleted) has a PII dimension, not merely a growth one.
- Proposed fix: Drop `cancellationReason` from the persisted whitelist and keep it DB-only, or encrypt the tombstone file with the existing token-cipher, and explicitly `chmod 0600` on create. Also correct the two comments that currently assert the opposite of what the code does.
- Confidence: Confirmed for the code path (verified on disk). Whether the production directory mode or the note contents make this exploitable in practice needs host access — see OQ-46.

**L-30. Userbot bootstrap logs the account phone number**
- Severity: Low
- Evidence: `userbot-auth-port.ts:43-44` `constructor(phoneNumber: string) { super(\`Phone number ${phoneNumber} is banned from Telegram.\`); }` (verified on disk). That message is thrown on the ban path and propagates into `userbot-bootstrap-service.ts:174-179`, whose catch does `logger.error({ districtId, sessionId, err: errorMsg }, …)` with `errorMsg = err.message`. A plaintext phone number therefore reaches application logs.
- Failure scenario: Citizen/operator phone PII in logs; low blast radius, single CLI-driven call path.
- Proposed fix: Have `PhoneNumberBannedError` carry the number as a field and use a static message string.
- Confidence: Confirmed.

**L-31. The 5-minute backup-expiry sweep re-verifies a district in its normal 30-day window on every tick with no backoff**
- Severity: Low
- Evidence: `district-deletion-job-handler.ts:225-230` schedules the backup-expiry cron on `'*/5 * * * *'`; the handler at `:232-238` calls `processOverdueBackupExpiries`; the "not yet expired" branch at `district-deletion-service.ts:575-582` returns **without writing any state** (verified on disk: branch 2 returns the current tombstone unchanged). Combined with a verifier that shells out to `pgbackrest info` per call, a district sitting in its 30-day retention window is re-probed ~8,640 times.
- Failure scenario: Wasted subprocess spawns and I/O on a timer, with no saturation signal. Low impact; the concern is recurring cost and the absence of backoff, not correctness.
- Proposed fix: Record a `lastVerifiedAt` / next-check timestamp on the tombstone and skip districts verified within their window, or widen the cron interval for the unexpired case.
- Confidence: Probable — the schedule, the handler and the no-write branch were verified, but the sweep's own SELECT predicate (whether it pre-filters to overdue rows only) was **not** read, so the true cost depends on it. See OQ-45. If it pre-filters, this downgrades to informational.

**Existing findings this phase CONFIRMS, EXTENDS, ANSWERS or REFUTES (no new IDs allocated)**

- **C-1 — CONFIRMED, still present, EXTENDED.** `token-cipher.ts:26-34` still resolves `ENCRYPTION_KEY` lazily on first use with the hardcoded `FALLBACK_DEV_KEY` at `:17` and no boot-time assertion. New mechanism: the same lazy resolver is the key source for the webhook HMAC (`webhook-security.ts:2`, `:12`), so a non-production `NODE_ENV` simultaneously yields a public AES key **and** a public webhook-auth key — it compounds H-19(2) rather than being a separate issue.
- **F-2 / H-5 plaintext `apiHash` — CONFIRMED, still present, EXTENDED.** `userbot-session-service.ts:193` inserts `apiHash: params.apiHash?.trim() || null` and `:285` returns it, against a plaintext `text('api_hash')` column (`district-telegram-userbot-sessions.ts:14`). Extension: the same table also stores `phoneNumber` as plaintext (`:12`) beside `apiId` (`:13`), next to the one field that **is** encrypted (`sessionEncrypted`/`sessionIv`/`sessionTag`, `:15-17`) — so the blast radius is api_hash + phone + api_id in cleartext.
- **F-3 key-version-never-consulted — CONFIRMED, still present, EXTENDED.** `token-cipher.ts:102-105` `decryptToken` takes only `{encryptedToken, tokenIv, tokenTag}` with no key-version parameter, while `:93` writes `tokenKeyVersion` and both `district-telegram-bots.ts:18` and `district-telegram-userbot-sessions.ts:18` persist it. `customKey` is plumbed through but never set by any `src/` caller, so it is always `undefined` in practice. Extension: this now applies to **two** tables (bot tokens and userbot sessions). Rotation remains impossible; F-3 stands in substance.
- **F-4 master-key reuse — CONFIRMED, no change.** `webhook-security.ts:12-13` derives the webhook secret as `HMAC-SHA256(masterKey, botId)` — the same key that AES-GCM-encrypts bot tokens and session strings serves a second cryptographic purpose.
- **F-6 webhook secret printed to stdout — CONFIRMED, still present.** `cli/register-webhook.ts:42` prints the payload including `secret_token` (set at `:35`), and `:45`/`:47` interpolate the decrypted bot token into URLs handed to `fetch`.
- **H-11 — NO CHANGE. The report's claim of a new seventh call site is REFUTED.** `district-deletion-job-handler.ts:177-181` is already named in H-11 (Phase 2.3 recorded `:141-145` **and** `:177-181`). A repo-wide grep for `clearPendingRetryFlag(` finds 8 call sites — `semantic-relevance-job-handler.ts:749`, `topic-assignment-coordinator.ts:780`, `topic-projection-job-handler.ts:470`, `burst-debounce-job-handler.ts:341`, `retention-job-handler.ts:160`, `qualification-job-handler.ts:161`, `district-deletion-job-handler.ts:143` and `:179` — all already accounted for by H-11. No extension.
- **M-35 `updateUserbotSessionStatus` allowlist — CONFIRMED as written; the pointer at backlog:1090 is ANSWERED.** `userbot-session-service.ts:443-445` still assigns `patch.status = updates.status` with no membership test against the status union, and the DB check constraint (`district-telegram-userbot-sessions.ts:25-28`, verified on disk) only constrains the **value set**, not the transition graph — so `BANNED → ACTIVE` passes both layers. M-35 is Confirmed, not merely probable.
- **M-37 leaked MTProto sockets — CONFIRMED as written, but moot at runtime** given H-22 (no connection is ever established).
- **OQ-37 (GramJS error text embedding secrets) — PARTIALLY ANSWERED.** The GramJS half stays unverifiable because the library is not installed (H-22). But one leak is confirmed **independent** of GramJS: the phone number, via `PhoneNumberBannedError` (L-30).
- **Pointer at backlog:1091 (userbot `sessionHash → districtId` cross-district guard) — ANSWERED: the guard is real and works as intended.** `userbot-connection-manager.ts:203-211` hashes the decrypted session string, checks `activeSessionKeys`, and rejects when the same auth key is already active for a **different** district. Caveat: it is an in-process memory map (`:58`), so with more than one userbot replica it would not be authoritative — but there is exactly one `userbot` service (`docker-compose.prod.yml:85-108`). No finding.
- **M-24 (tombstone written after COMMIT, compensating issue row non-transactional) — CONFIRMED structurally and EXTENDED.** `district-deletion-service.ts:333` closes the transaction, then `:337-339` writes the tombstone, then `:350-396` inserts/updates the `operationalIssues` row with a `.catch()` that only logs (`:387-396`). The `recommendedAction` text at `:364` even instructs the operator to check `tombstones.json` and disk permissions, confirming the file is treated as a first-class artifact despite having neither a transaction nor a cross-process lock — exactly the gap M-41 exploits.
- **Pointer at backlog:1095 (debug-payload purge genuinely overwrites) — ANSWERED / REFUTED as a concern.** `debug-payload-retention.ts:21-36` replaces the whole `raw_payload` column with a 4-key `jsonb_build_object` whitelist, so `verbatimText` is destroyed, not merely omitted from a read projection. The predicate-narrowness concern there is L-22, already recorded — not double-counted.
- **ADR 0009 / AR-2 — RE-CONFIRMED, with a further weakened containment claim.** Transport risks remain correctly accepted; the structure matches the ADR (dedicated userbot service per ADR 0009:22 → `docker-compose.prod.yml:85-108`, passive-only port, unified intake core). But H-22 means that in the deployed container **no MTProto connection is ever made**, so ADR 0009:28's "automated ban detection transitions session status to BANNED" and :30's "abnormal signals raise warning alerts" cannot fire at all. The acceptance still stands (bans are still an inherent risk), but fixing H-22 and H-16/M-35 should be treated as preserving the ADR's validity, not as new scope. Recommend annotating the AR-2 entry accordingly.
- **ADR 0007 (stateful sessions) — noted, not re-litigated.** ADR 0007:18 claims district suspension "immediately purges or invalidates active sessions"; consistent with Phase 1.1 M-3, the session store (`schema/sessions.ts`) has no district linkage at all — it keys on `accountId` only. The storage layer therefore provides no mechanism for that ADR promise.
- **ADR-Accepted Risks — NO storage/crypto-specific ADR exists.** There is no accepted-risk decision covering the plaintext `apiHash`/`phoneNumber`, the master-key dual use, or the unencrypted tombstone file — so M-42 is a finding, not an accepted risk.

**VERIFIED SOUND**

- **AES-256-GCM is implemented correctly.** `token-cipher.ts:74-96` `encryptToken` uses `crypto.randomBytes(12)` **per call** (`:80`) — no fixed or reused IV, no nonce reuse; `createCipheriv('aes-256-gcm', key, iv)` (`:81`); tag extracted via `getAuthTag()` (`:87`) and persisted separately (`:92`). `decryptToken` (`:102-120`) calls `decipher.setAuthTag(tag)` (`:112`) **before** `final()` (`:116`), so GCM authentication is genuinely enforced and tampering with ciphertext or tag throws rather than returning plaintext. This **refutes** the unauthenticated-encryption hypothesis for this codebase. (Re-verified line by line by the orchestrator.)
- **Key derivation is strict.** `getEncryptionKey` (`:26-52`) accepts hex-64 / base64-44 / raw-32 only and throws on any other length (`:45-49`); it refuses the dev fallback when `NODE_ENV === 'production'` (`:30-32`). The primitive is sound; the fallback exposure is the C-1 environment issue, not a key-derivation weakness.
- **No non-constant-time secret comparison.** `webhook-security.ts:34-37` SHA-256s **both** sides to fixed 32-byte buffers before `crypto.timingSafeEqual` — the correct pattern, avoiding both the length-mismatch `RangeError` and length/prefix side channels.
- **Argon2 config is sound.** `argon2.ts:3-8`: argon2id, memoryCost 65536 (64 MB), timeCost 3, parallelism 1 (deliberately 1 to avoid libuv worker-pool contention). `verifyPassword` (`:14-19`) swallows errors and returns `false` rather than leaking a distinguishable failure.
- **Temporary password generation is CSPRNG-based and policy-checked.** `temporary-password.ts:15` uses `crypto.randomInt` (not `Math.random`); Fisher-Yates with `randomInt` (`:23-31`); guarantees all four character classes (`:50-55`); excludes homoglyphs; validates against the policy/blocklist with up to 10 attempts (`:49-69`).
- **The external tombstone store FAILS CLOSED and writes atomically.** `external-tombstone-store.ts:79-116`: missing file → `[]`; empty → `[]`; JSON parse error → `TombstoneStoreCorruptedError` (`:92-95`); schema validation failure → `TombstoneStoreCorruptedError` (`:99-103`). No silent no-op on corruption. Writes use a unique temp path with pid + timestamp + 4 random bytes (`:122-125`), `writeFile` then `handle.sync()` (fsync) before close (`:132-133`), then atomic rename (`:143`) with bounded backoff for Windows `EPERM`/`EBUSY`/`EACCES` (`:146-156`), and temp-file cleanup on failure (`:163`). A genuinely well-built atomic writer — its only flaw is the process-scoped lock (M-41). (Re-verified by the orchestrator.)
- **No path traversal in any file-writing adapter.** Every write/append/stream/mkdir/unlink in `apps/backend/src` was grepped: the only production filesystem writer is `external-tombstone-store.ts` (`:120` mkdir, `:132` writeFile, `:163` unlink), and its path comes from `TOMBSTONE_STORE_PATH` or a cwd-relative constant (`:52-56`), never from request input. The only other fs reads are two CLIs reading stdin (fd 0). No request-derived path is ever written.
- **Sessions store only a hash.** `schema/sessions.ts:11` `tokenHash` is the only credential column (no plaintext token), with a unique index and an explicit supporting index (`:19`). Consistent with Phase 1.1's CSPRNG/SHA-256 findings.
- **The userbot session STRING (the MTProto auth key, effectively a bearer credential) IS encrypted at rest.** `userbot-session-service.ts:171-181` (create), `userbot-bootstrap-service.ts:131-147` (activate) and `userbot-session-service.ts:451-467` (update) all route the session string through `encryptToken`. Public formatting strips it: `userbot-session-service.ts:119-122` exposes only `hasSession: boolean`, and `userbot-session-routes.ts:21-43` `formatPublicSession` omits `sessionEncrypted`/`sessionIv`/`sessionTag`. No API response returns the session string. (This is the one credential class the plaintext `apiHash`/`phoneNumber` sits beside — the asymmetry is the finding, not the encryption.)
- **ADR 0009's service-isolation claim (:22) is honoured.** `docker-compose.prod.yml:85-108` defines a dedicated `userbot` service running `src/entrypoints/userbot.ts`, and GramJS is confined behind `modules/userbot/userbot-client-port.ts` + `adapters/telegram/*`. Hexagonal isolation (ADR 0001) holds structurally.

---

### Layer 4 — Runtime, Ops & Deployment

#### Phase 4.1 — HTTP Hardening

Phase verdict: The HTTP layer's **input** boundary is deliberate and mostly correct — the origin guard fails closed, the webhook secret comparison is constant-time, the cookie flags match on set and clear, and the 5xx message path is genuinely sanitized. The defects are all at the **server lifecycle and output** boundaries. One High: no timeout is configured anywhere, and the pinned Fastify version writes `requestTimeout: 0`, `connectionTimeout: 0` and `handlerTimeout: 0` straight onto the Node server, so three independent bounds that would otherwise cap a slow-request attack are off. The Mediums are a blanket `trustProxy: true`, a self-referential `Origin == Host` acceptance path, the absence of any response allowlist on 82 of 87 route registrations, and the total absence of a Content-Security-Policy.

**Counts: 0 Critical, 1 High, 4 Medium, 3 Low.**

**Method.** This phase was audited by one agent and then independently re-verified by a second, adversarial agent working from the same brief. Both passes were read-only: no build, no typecheck, no tests, no network, no database, no git. The verifier re-counted every load-bearing number from source, read the pinned dependency's own source under `node_modules`, and checked all proposed IDs against the existing backlog for collisions and duplication. This section applies the verifier's verdicts: one proposed Low was REFUTED and replaced by the inverse defect, one Medium was withdrawn as a duplicate, two proposed Lows were partial overlaps and were narrowed or folded into an existing finding, and the High gained a third disabled timeout bound. **Every path, line number and dependency version below was re-derived against the files on disk and the pinned package before being written** — see the reconciliation note at the end of this phase.

**Route × Control Matrix.** 87 route registrations were enumerated across 16 `*-routes.ts` files under `apps/backend/src`. Fastify's `exposeHeadRoutes` default is `true` (`node_modules/.pnpm/fastify@5.12.0/node_modules/fastify/lib/config-validator.js:1265`), so each GET registration also answers HEAD; those are not listed separately. Re-counted from source: **40 of the 87 are GET** (39 POST, 4 PATCH, 3 DELETE, 1 PUT), giving 40 additional HEAD routes.

Legend — **Origin**: `R` = inherits the root-level `verifyStateChangingOrigin` hook (registered at `auth-routes.ts:20`, applies app-wide per Phase 1.2 M-5); `R+D` = root hook plus a redundant per-scope re-registration; `EX` = exempted by the path-prefix check at `origin-guard.ts:48`; `PUB` = registered directly on the root instance and additionally covered by the root hook for its exempt GET method.
**Rate**: `–` = none; `RL` = the DB-backed sign-in limiter only.
**AuthN/AuthZ**: `PO` = `createRequireProductOwner`; `HOKIM` = `createRequireHokim`; `DA` = `createRequireDistrictAccess`; `SESS` = hand-rolled cookie check inside the handler; `SEC` = `x-telegram-bot-api-secret-token` check; `PUB` = public.
**In-schema**: Fastify `body:`/`querystring:` schema declared in the route options. **Out-schema**: Fastify `response:` schema.

| Route | Origin | Rate | AuthN | AuthZ | In-schema | Out-schema |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| POST /api/v1/auth/sign-in | R | RL | credential | – | – | – |
| POST /api/v1/auth/sign-out | R | – | SESS (optional) | – | – | – |

**Matrix totals (OBSERVED).** Origin guard: 85 routes inherit the root hook; **10 scopes additionally re-register it redundantly** (11 `addHook` call sites in total, counting the root registration at `auth-routes.ts:20` — backlog L-7); 2 webhook routes are exempted by the path-prefix check; 3 public health probes are registered directly on the root and are therefore also covered by the root hook for their exempt GET method. Rate limit: **1 of 87** routes. Authentication: 78 require a session; 4 are the `/auth/*` family; 3 are public health probes; 2 are secret-header webhooks. Fastify-declared input schemas: **22 `schema:` blocks in 4 files** — `admin-signals-routes.ts` (6 body, 1 querystring), `hokim-topics-routes.ts` (3 body, 4 querystring), `district-topics-routes.ts` (1 body, 2 querystring), `health-routes.ts` (0 body, 0 querystring, 5 response). Fastify-declared **response** schemas: **5 blocks, all in `health-routes.ts`** — i.e. **5 of 87 route registrations have an output allowlist, and three of those five are unauthenticated.** The other 82 serialize whatever the handler returns.

**In-handler zod validation (OBSERVED, in addition to the table):** `auth-routes.ts:24,142`; `telegram-groups-routes.ts:110`; `audit-routes.ts:30,66`; `subscriptions-routes.ts:113`; `userbot-session-routes.ts:122`; `issue-routes.ts:282`. `telegram-intake-routes.ts` performs none — it reads raw `req.body`. For the remaining routes no handler body was read, so no claim is made either way.

---

##### High

**H-23. No request, connection or handler timeout is configured anywhere, and Fastify explicitly disables three of Node's built-in bounds — unauthenticated slow-request socket exhaustion**

- Severity: High
- Evidence: `apps/backend/src/entrypoints/http.ts:93-101` — the `Fastify({...})` call passes only `logger` and `trustProxy: true`. Every other option falls to Fastify's defaults, read from the pinned source at `node_modules/.pnpm/fastify@5.12.0/node_modules/fastify/lib/config-validator.js:1265`: `connectionTimeout:0, keepAliveTimeout:72000, maxRequestsPerSocket:0, requestTimeout:0, handlerTimeout:0, bodyLimit:1048576, onProtoPoisoning:"error", onConstructorPoisoning:"error", exposeHeadRoutes:true`. `node_modules/.pnpm/fastify@5.12.0/node_modules/fastify/lib/server.js:339-341` then assigns those values onto the Node server by bare assignment: `server.keepAliveTimeout = options.keepAliveTimeout; server.requestTimeout = options.requestTimeout; server.setTimeout(options.connectionTimeout)`. There is no "use Node default if zero" translation for `requestTimeout` — the only option carrying a `> 0` guard is `maxRequestsPerSocket` (`server.js:342-345`). So `requestTimeout = 0` is written straight onto the Node server and disables Node's own 5-minute request bound; `setTimeout(0)` disables the socket-inactivity bound; and `handlerTimeout: 0` means no handler-duration ceiling. `config-validator.js:1265` contains **no `headersTimeout` key at all** — it is not a Fastify option, so Node's own 60 s default applies unchanged and there is no Fastify-imposed `headersTimeout` defect. At the edge, `deploy/compose/Caddyfile` (45 lines, read in full) declares no `servers { timeouts { ... } }` block.
- Mechanism: An unauthenticated attacker opens TCP connections to the published edge and, after sending complete headers with `Content-Length: 1048576` (the global body limit), trickles the body at a few bytes per minute. Node never times the request out (`requestTimeout = 0`), the socket never idles out (`connectionTimeout = 0`), and `maxRequestsPerSocket` is unlimited, so each connection is pinned indefinitely. The global body limit bounds bytes per request but not *time per request*, and `keepAliveTimeout` of 72 s — which is **longer** than Node's 5 s default — only applies between requests, not during one. Caddy holds a matching upstream connection for each, doubling the pinned-socket cost.
- Failure scenario: A single host with a few thousand open sockets exhausts the single backend container's connection and memory budget and, on the way, consumes Caddy's upstream pool. Unlike H-1 (Argon2 work amplification) and H-17 (ingestion flooding), this needs no credentials, no valid payload, and no knowledge of the API surface, and it wedges the whole citizen-facing service rather than one path. Every request the app would otherwise serve, including sign-in, is starved. It would not surface as a health failure until the sockets were already exhausted: `/api/v1/health/live` answers as long as the event loop turns, and `/api/v1/health/ready` probes only the database, the queue and restore reconciliation.
- Proposed fix: Pass explicit `requestTimeout`, `connectionTimeout` and `handlerTimeout` values to `Fastify({...})` at `entrypoints/http.ts:93-101` — e.g. `requestTimeout: 30000`, `connectionTimeout: 30000`, `handlerTimeout: 60000` — and lower `keepAliveTimeout` from its 72 s default toward Node's 5 s. Optionally add a `servers { timeouts { read_body ... } }` block to the Caddyfile as a second layer. Declare `ulimits` (or another fd ceiling) on the `backend` service in `deploy/compose/docker-compose.prod.yml` so the exhaustion ceiling is bounded and known.
- Confidence: Confirmed for the construction, the defaults and the assignment (all re-derived from the pinned source by both passes). The failure scenario is a sound inference from those facts, not an observed attack. Caddy's own compiled-in defaults and the host file-descriptor limit were not measured from the repo, so the practical ceiling is unquantified.

##### Medium

**M-43. `trustProxy: true` is unconditional — `req.ip` is derived from an unvalidated `X-Forwarded-For` chain, and it is the sign-in limiter's key material**

- Severity: Medium
- Evidence: `apps/backend/src/entrypoints/http.ts:100` — `trustProxy: true`, with no environment gate. `apps/backend/src/modules/auth/rate-limiter.ts:14-18` — `buildRateLimitKey(ip, username)` returns `` `${cleanIp}:${cleanUser}` ``. `apps/backend/src/modules/auth/auth-routes.ts:35-42` reads `req.ip` and falls back to the literal `'127.0.0.1'` when it is empty. `deploy/compose/Caddyfile:19` — `header_up X-Forwarded-For {remote_host}`, which **overwrites** rather than appends. `deploy/compose/docker-compose.prod.yml:20-53` — the `backend` service declares no `ports:` mapping; only `caddy` publishes (`:117-119`).
- Mechanism: `trustProxy: true` tells Fastify to trust *every* hop in the forwarding chain, so `req.ip` is taken from the leftmost `X-Forwarded-For` entry rather than from the socket peer. That is correct today only because Caddy overwrites the header and because the backend container is unpublished — both are configuration properties, not code. The setting itself cannot be narrowed without editing source. Any request path that reaches the backend container without traversing Caddy's `handle /api/*` block — a future `ports:` mapping, a `network_mode: host` change, another container on the compose network, or an operator tunnel — lets the caller set `X-Forwarded-For` freely. `buildRateLimitKey` then keys the sign-in lockout on attacker-supplied text, so each request can present a fresh fabricated IP and defeat the lockout entirely; the `'127.0.0.1'` fallback conversely collapses every empty-`req.ip` request into one shared counter. The same value is written into audit records via `getClientInfo` (`apps/backend/src/modules/auth/client-info.ts:12`), and via `districts-routes.ts:59,99,170,201`, `telegram-groups-routes.ts:200,234,257,297,340`, `subscriptions-routes.ts:130,178,236,305,371,419,497,569` and `telegram-bot-routes.ts:64,83`.
- Failure scenario: The deployed topology is sound today — this is a defense-in-depth gap, not a live bypass, and it does not contradict the verified-sound entry at backlog L126. But it is a single configuration edit away from making the sign-in lockout and every audit-recorded client IP attacker-controlled, with no code-level signal that anything changed.
- Proposed fix: Replace the blanket `trustProxy: true` with an explicit hop count or an IP/CIDR trust list restricted to the Caddy container, driven by an environment variable, and fail loudly at boot if the value is unset in production. Log a startup warning whenever `req.ip` falls back to the literal `'127.0.0.1'` more than once.
- Confidence: Confirmed for the setting, the key derivation, the Caddy overwrite and the unpublished backend (all re-derived on disk). Live or latent depends entirely on OQ-48.

**M-44. The origin guard accepts `Origin` equal to the request's own `Host` header, making the check self-referential**

- Severity: Medium
- Evidence: `apps/backend/src/modules/auth/origin-guard.ts:13-25` — `isOriginAllowed` evaluates the `APP_ORIGIN` allowlist loop at `:13-21` first; when no allowlist entry matches, control falls through to the `Host` comparison at `:23-25`, which returns `true` when `originUrl.host.toLowerCase() === hostHeader.toLowerCase()`. A request whose `Origin` fails the allowlist is therefore still admitted via the `Host` arm. `deploy/compose/Caddyfile:17` — `header_up Host {host}` forwards a client-supplied `Host` verbatim. `apps/backend/src/modules/auth/origin-guard.ts:28-32` is the second self-referential arm: any request whose `Origin` hostname is `localhost` or `127.0.0.1` and whose `Host` starts with `localhost` or `127.0.0.1` is trusted.
- Mechanism: The `Host` header is whatever the client sent, so `Origin: https://evil.com` + `Host: evil.com` passes `origin-guard.ts:24` and the state-changing request is admitted. The `APP_ORIGIN` allowlist — the only strictly-bounded arm of the function — is never consulted for such requests. The guard is attached at the root (`auth-routes.ts:20`) and inherited by 85 routes.
- Failure scenario: Purely theoretical as deployed. A browser derives `Host` from the URL it is fetching and `Origin` from its own page, so a page on `attacker.com` fetching `https://mahalla-ovozi.uz` sends `Origin: https://attacker.com` with `Host: mahalla-ovozi.uz` — a mismatch that 403s. Producing a matching pair requires a non-browser client that can set both — and such a client holding the session cookie does not need CSRF at all. Caddy is the current practical barrier: a request carrying `Host: evil.com` matches none of its site blocks and never reaches the backend. The residual risk is that the application's CSRF posture reduces to "Caddy's site-block matching works", which is an edge-configuration property rather than an application control.
- Proposed fix: Remove the `originUrl.host === hostHeader` short-circuit from `isOriginAllowed` and rely solely on the `APP_ORIGIN` allowlist; or keep it but require that `hostHeader` also appear in `APP_ORIGIN`. Drop the `localhost`/`127.0.0.1` arm unless `NODE_ENV !== 'production'`.
- Confidence: Confirmed mechanism (re-derived on disk), theoretical exploit — the verifier reached the same judgement as the auditor.

**M-45. No response allowlist anywhere except the public health probes — 82 of 87 route registrations serialize handler return values unfiltered**

- Severity: Medium
- Evidence: Counting every `schema:` block in the backend's route files gives **22 blocks in exactly four files**. Of those, only `apps/backend/src/modules/health/health-routes.ts` declares `response:` — five blocks at `:147`, `:168`, `:215`, `:256`, `:280`, covering `/api/v1/health/live`, `/api/v1/health/ready`, `/api/v1/health`, `/api/v1/health/system` and `/api/v1/districts/:districtId/health`. The remaining 82 route registrations have no `response` schema, so Fastify applies no output filtering and `reply.send(value)` transmits the object exactly as constructed. Confirmed at two representative handlers: `apps/backend/src/modules/audit/audit-routes.ts:52` sends the pagination page straight from `auditQueryService.queryAuditEvents`, and `:94` sends the event detail straight from `getAuditEventById`.
- Mechanism: With no `response` schema there is no serialization boundary at all, so the wire shape of these endpoints is whatever the service layer happens to return. A column added to `audit_events`, `accepted_evidence`, `topics`, `topic_projections`, `ai_operations` or `accounts` is exposed to every caller of the corresponding route in the same deploy that adds it, with no second place to notice. The contracts in `packages/api-contracts` exist for many of these endpoints but not one is wired as a Fastify `response` schema, so the contract layer and the wire layer have no mechanical link.
- Failure scenario: A missing defense-in-depth control, not a confirmed leak — no route was found that currently returns a field it should not (the district and Hokim topic routes are *supposed* to return evidence text to their authorized callers). Its severity rests on the size of the unaudited surface and the data class: 82 endpoints over citizen-derived tables where a schema change reaches the client automatically.
- Proposed fix: Wire the existing `packages/api-contracts` response types into Fastify `response` schemas for the highest-value read paths first (audit, admin signals, topics, ai-operations, districts), and add a CI check that fails when a route is registered without a `response` schema unless it carries an explicit opt-out comment.
- Confidence: Confirmed for the structural absence (re-counted on disk by both passes). The per-route consequences are INFERRED — 82 handler bodies were not read, so this asserts the absence of a control, not any specific field leak.

**M-46. No Content-Security-Policy anywhere, and the backend emits no security headers of its own**

- Severity: Medium
- Evidence: `deploy/compose/Caddyfile:6-12` sets `Strict-Transport-Security`, `X-Content-Type-Options`, `X-Frame-Options`, `Referrer-Policy` and `Permissions-Policy` on every handle, including `/api/*`. It sets no `Content-Security-Policy`. A repo-wide search for `Content-Security-Policy|default-src|script-src|frame-ancestors|helmet` over `apps/`, `packages/` and `deploy/` returns **no matches at all**. On the application side, `@fastify/helmet` is **absent**: `apps/backend/package.json:26-39` lists only `@fastify/compress`, `@fastify/cookie` and `@fastify/cors` plus non-security packages, and `node_modules/.pnpm/` contains no `@fastify+helmet` entry. The same probe confirms `@fastify/rate-limit`, `@fastify/csrf` and `@fastify/under-pressure` are likewise absent, while `@fastify+compress@9.2.0`, `@fastify+cookie@11.1.2` and `@fastify+cors@11.3.0` are present. `apps/backend/src/entrypoints/http.ts:93-173` registers only cookie, compress and cors, and its error and not-found handlers (`:151-173`) set no headers beyond the envelope body.
- Mechanism: The SPA is served by Caddy's `file_server` (`Caddyfile:25-34`) with no CSP, so a script-injection defect anywhere in the web client executes with full origin privileges — including the ability to call every `/api/*` route with the victim's session cookie, since the cookie is `sameSite: 'strict'` but *same-origin* requests are exactly what XSS produces. There is no second line of defense between an injected script and the authenticated API. Separately, every response the backend emits directly — including the 500 and 404 envelopes, which do carry JSON — is served header-less by the app and relies entirely on Caddy's `header` block applying, which it does only for traffic that traverses Caddy.
- Failure scenario: A missing defense-in-depth control over an application whose entire authenticated surface is cookie-based and same-origin. The consequence is not a bypass of any existing control but the absence of containment if one fails — and the failure mode (an injected script issuing authenticated API calls that exfiltrate citizen data) is precisely the one CSP is designed to bound. A CORS policy cannot substitute: the injected script is same-origin, so no CORS check is ever reached.
- Proposed fix: Add a `Content-Security-Policy` header to the `header` block at `deploy/compose/Caddyfile:6-12`, starting in report-only mode against the real SPA bundle and then enforcing — `default-src 'self'; script-src 'self'; object-src 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'; base-uri 'self'`. Register `@fastify/helmet` on the backend root as a second layer so that API responses carry their own headers rather than depending on the edge. Note `X-Frame-Options: DENY` is already set, so `frame-ancestors` is a belt-and-braces addition rather than the primary control.
- Confidence: Confirmed (the header block, the plugin absence and the repo-wide search were all re-derived on disk). Whether the SPA bundle can run under a strict `script-src 'self'` was not evaluated — the web client was out of scope for this phase, and that evaluation is the first step of the proposed fix.

##### Low

**L-32. A 4xx path forwards the raw V8 JSON parser message to the client**

- Severity: Low
- Evidence: `apps/backend/src/entrypoints/http.ts:110-121` registers a custom `application/json` content-type parser with `{ parseAs: 'string' }` and, on failure, calls `done(err as Error, undefined)` with the bare `SyntaxError` from `JSON.parse`. Under `parseAs: 'string'` the request travels Fastify's `rawBody` path, whose `onEnd` handler at `node_modules/.pnpm/fastify@5.12.0/node_modules/fastify/lib/content-type-parser.js:289-290` assigns `err.statusCode = 400` whenever the thrown value does not already carry a 4xx-or-higher `statusCode`. `resolveStatusCode` at `apps/backend/src/modules/errors/api-error-envelope.ts:114-120` then sees an integer in 400–599 and returns 400. `resolveErrorMessage` at `:130-136` takes the `statusCode < 500` branch and returns `error.message` unchanged.
- Mechanism: Because the status is 400 and not 500, the message is *not* scrubbed. The client receives V8's own parser text verbatim — e.g. `Unexpected token } in JSON at position 5`, or any other string `JSON.parse` chooses to emit. This is a message-disclosure path, not a server fault.
- Failure scenario: Low blast radius. The disclosed string is a client-supplied-payload parser message, so it carries no server state; the real cost is that internal parser wording becomes part of the public API contract, and an attacker learns which JSON engine version is in use. It is recorded because the hardening posture elsewhere in this envelope is explicitly to never echo thrown text to a client on an unexpected path.
- Proposed fix: In the custom parser's `catch` block at `http.ts:118-120`, wrap the `SyntaxError` in a typed application error carrying a fixed client-safe message (and `statusCode: 400`), rather than passing the raw error to `done`.
- Confidence: Confirmed (re-derived from the pinned parser source and the envelope on disk). **This finding replaces the auditor's original proposed Low, which claimed the opposite outcome (a `500 INTERNAL_ERROR`); the verifier refuted that claim and this is its recorded correction.**

**L-33. The custom JSON content-type parser displaces Fastify's prototype-poisoning-guarded parser**

- Severity: Low
- Evidence: Fastify's built-in JSON parser is built on `secure-json-parse` with `protoAction` and `constructorAction` both defaulting to `'error'` — `node_modules/.pnpm/fastify@5.12.0/node_modules/fastify/lib/content-type-parser.js:312-328` (`getDefaultJsonParser`, with `parseOptions = { protoAction, constructorAction }` at `:313` passed to `secureJsonParse` at `:323`), fed from `config-validator.js:51-56` and `:1265` (`onProtoPoisoning: "error"`, `onConstructorPoisoning: "error"`). `apps/backend/src/entrypoints/http.ts:110-121` replaces that parser for `application/json` with a direct `JSON.parse` (`:116`) that passes no action options, so a body containing `__proto__` or `constructor` keys is accepted where the default would have rejected it, while `onProtoPoisoning`/`onConstructorPoisoning` remain set and silently inert for this content type.
- Mechanism: `JSON.parse` defines `__proto__` as an own data property rather than walking the prototype chain, so a parsed body alone does not pollute anything — there is no pollution in isolation, and no recursive merge was found on the path. The exposure is the *removal of a guard* the rest of the codebase still believes is active, on an endpoint set that spreads parsed bodies into downstream objects. If any consumer ever performs an object merge or assignment with that parsed body, the second half of the classic prototype-pollution chain is already available.
- Failure scenario: Not exploitable as the code stands. Recorded because the guard's absence is invisible: the config still says `"error"`, the parser no longer honours it, and nothing in the code or tests marks the discrepancy.
- Proposed fix: Delete the custom parser and rely on Fastify's default (the handler comment says it exists to tolerate empty bodies — handle that case with a schema default or a `preValidation` hook instead), or call `secure-json-parse` directly with the same `protoAction`/`constructorAction` options the default uses.
- Confidence: Confirmed for the displacement — `ContentTypeParser.prototype.existingParser` (`content-type-parser.js:105-117`) returns `customParsers.get('application/json').fn !== this[kDefaultJsonParse]`, which is `false` for the pre-seeded default and `true` only once a user parser replaces it, so `addContentTypeParser` genuinely overrides rather than throwing `FST_ERR_CTP_ALREADY_PRESENT`. The `JSON.parse` semantics and the inert-config half are also observed.

**L-34. The development compose file publishes PostgreSQL to the host with default credentials, and an identical second copy exists at the repo root**

- Severity: Low
- Evidence: `deploy/compose/docker-compose.yml:8` — `POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:-mahalla_dev_password}`; `:10-11` — `ports: - "${POSTGRES_PORT:-5433}:5432"`; the `userbot` service at `:20-37` sets `NODE_ENV: development` at `:32` and embeds the same default password in `DATABASE_URL` at `:34`. The production stack is the sibling `deploy/compose/docker-compose.prod.yml`, whose `postgres` service (`:2-18`) declares **no `ports:` key** and uses the fail-fast form `${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?Database password required}` at `:8`. **The distinct part of this finding:** `docker-compose.yml` at the repo root (21 lines) is a near-duplicate of the dev file — same `postgres:18-alpine` image, the same `mahalla_dev_password` default at `:8`, and the same `"${POSTGRES_PORT:-5433}:5432"` publish at `:10-11`.
- Mechanism: Per C-1, a non-production `NODE_ENV` is the condition that activates the hardcoded fallback encryption key, and the `userbot` service in the `deploy/` copy runs with `NODE_ENV: development`. Its position under `deploy/` also makes it plausible to an operator as a deployment artifact. An operator who starts either copy on a host reachable from the internet both exposes the database on 5433 with a repository-visible password and runs the userbot with the public key.
- Failure scenario: Low, because starting a dev compose file on an internet-reachable host requires an operator error — but the error is a single plausible command, and the consequence is a fully exposed database plus a public encryption key.
- Proposed fix: Change both files to `${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:?Database password required}` and remove the host port publish from both, matching the production stack. If the root copy is dead, delete it; if it is live, document which one operators are meant to run.
- Confidence: Confirmed (all three files re-read on disk). **Narrowed by the verifier:** the default-password half of this finding is already recorded as F-12, which cites `deploy/compose/docker-compose.yml:8`. Only the published host port and the unrecorded root-level second copy are new. **Folded in from a withdrawn proposal:** the authenticated non-ingestion surface has no request ceiling of any kind — `POST /api/v1/auth/change-first-login-password` (which runs an Argon2 verify), `POST /api/v1/auth/sign-out`, and every Product-Owner and Hokim mutation route have no counter. Impact is bounded because a valid session is required for all of them. The same fail-closed origin guard that makes this surface hard to abuse also makes it unreachable to any non-browser consumer — a curl script, a monitoring probe, a native mobile client or a service integration that does not fabricate a browser `Origin` cannot authenticate at all. Both facts belong to the existing H-17 / L123 entries and are restated here only so this phase's coverage is complete.

##### Verified sound

- **The 5xx error path leaks nothing.** `resolveErrorMessage` (`apps/backend/src/modules/errors/api-error-envelope.ts:130-136`) returns the fixed `INTERNAL_ERROR_MESSAGE` for every `statusCode >= 500`, so raw driver messages, SQL text and stack traces cannot reach the client through it. Stack traces and the full error object go to `request.log.error` (`apps/backend/src/entrypoints/http.ts:153-161`), which is server-side only.
- **Fastify survives a throwing error handler — the envelope's `parse` cannot hang a request.** `node_modules/.pnpm/fastify@5.12.0/node_modules/fastify/lib/error-handler.js:48-49` sets `reply[kReplyNextErrorHandler] = Object.getPrototypeOf(errorHandler)` before invoking the handler, and `:63-75` wraps the call in `try { … } catch (err) { reply.send(err) }`, so a throw inside `setErrorHandler` re-dispatches to the prototype chain and terminates in `defaultErrorHandler` (`:78-83`). Backlog pointer L1171 asked whether `ApiErrorEnvelopeSchema.parse` inside the handler (`api-error-envelope.ts:143`, `:186`) could hang or double-error; **it cannot.** Reading both `parse` call sites additionally shows the inputs are constructed so the parse cannot fail: `buildValidationError` supplies a literal code, a message that `toValidationErrorItem` guarantees non-empty, and already shape-filtered items; `buildGenericError` supplies `resolveErrorCode`'s non-empty fallback and `resolveErrorMessage`'s non-empty fallback, with `blockers` filtered by `isPlainRecord` (`:173`) and `details` gated on `isPlainRecord` (`:177`).
- **`checkDbHealth`'s raw driver `error` string never reaches a client.** `apps/backend/src/adapters/db/client.ts:99-106` does populate `DbHealthResult.error` from `err.message`, but no consumer reads it. Backlog pointer L1170 asked for exactly this confirmation: **no route serialises that field.** There are three production call sites — `health-routes.ts:176`, `health-routes.ts:223`, and `apps/backend/src/modules/health/health-checker.ts:132` — and all three read only `probe.isHealthy`. `health-routes.ts:195-203` sends only the literal enums `'ok'`/`'down'`/`'ready'`/`'unready'`, and `:228-239` sends only `status`, `timestamp` and the hardcoded version. The field is dropped at the route boundary.
- **Cookie flags are correct and consistent.** `apps/backend/src/modules/auth/auth-routes.ts:52-58` sets `httpOnly: true`, `sameSite: 'strict'`, `path: '/'`, `secure: req.protocol === 'https'` and an explicit `expires`; the three `clearCookie` calls (`:88`, `:101`, `:128`) and the one in `require-auth.ts:39-44` repeat the same attributes, so the clear actually matches the set. There is no `domain` attribute, so the cookie cannot be widened to a parent domain. (M-1 and M-2 already record the missing `__Host-` prefix and the non-reissued expiry; the comment at `auth-routes.ts:51` refers to a `__Host-` rationale that the actual name at `session-manager.ts:9` — `process.env.SESSION_COOKIE_NAME || 'mahalla_session'` — does not carry by default. The flag-matching claim still holds; the comment's naming premise does not.)
- **CORS is allowlist-gated, not wildcarded, and `credentials: true` never meets a reflective wildcard.** `apps/backend/src/entrypoints/http.ts:136-147` passes a function to `origin`, so `@fastify/cors` sets `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` to the reflected origin **only after** the callback has returned `true`; a rejected origin goes to `cb(new ForbiddenOriginError(), false)`. The reflection is therefore bounded by `APP_ORIGIN`. A missing `Origin` is accepted (`:139`), which is correct for same-origin and non-browser traffic and does not create a CORS grant. The unset-`APP_ORIGIN` default is `http://localhost:5173` (`:134`), which fails *closed* for the real SPA rather than open.
- **Webhook secret comparison is constant-time and length-safe.** `apps/backend/src/modules/telegram-intake/webhook-security.ts:34-37` hashes both the expected secret and the presented header to 32-byte SHA-256 digests before `crypto.timingSafeEqual`, which removes both the length-disclosure channel and the `RangeError` on mismatched buffer lengths. Both webhook routes apply it in a `preHandler` before any handler code runs and each declares `bodyLimit: 262144` (`telegram-groups-routes.ts:85`, `telegram-intake-routes.ts:32`).
- **Body limits are set globally and not raised anywhere.** `entrypoints/http.ts:93-101` does not override `bodyLimit`, so Fastify's 1 MiB default applies to all 87 routes; the only two per-route overrides are the webhooks, and both *lower* the cap to 256 KiB. No route raises it.
- **Session token handling is sound at the HTTP layer.** Tokens are 256 bits of `crypto.randomBytes(32).toString('base64url')` (`apps/backend/src/modules/auth/session-manager.ts:11-14`), stored only as SHA-256 hashes (`:16-18`), never echoed in a response body, and revoked by hash on sign-out. The cookie is the only transport.
- **The not-found handler discloses nothing.** `serializeNotFoundError` (`api-error-envelope.ts:206-214`) returns a fixed `NOT_FOUND` envelope with no route list, no path echo and no stack.
- **The deployed edge overwrites rather than appends the client IP.** `deploy/compose/Caddyfile:19` uses `header_up X-Forwarded-For {remote_host}`, which replaces any client-supplied value, and `deploy/compose/docker-compose.prod.yml:20-53` gives the `backend` service no `ports:` mapping, so only Caddy can reach port 3000. This is why M-43 and M-44 are Medium rather than High, and it concurs with the verified-sound statement at backlog L126.
- **TLS termination and the header set that does exist are correct.** `deploy/compose/Caddyfile:38-44` provisions automatic ACME certificates for `mahalla-ovozi.uz` and `www.mahalla-ovozi.uz` and redirects the plain-HTTP VPS-IP listener to the canonical domain permanently; `:6-12` sets HSTS with `includeSubDomains; preload`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `X-Frame-Options: DENY`, `Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin` and a restrictive `Permissions-Policy`. The only gap is CSP (now M-46).
- **Authentication and authorization guards are present on every non-public route.** `apps/backend/src/modules/auth/require-auth.ts:21-182` validates the cookie, touches the session, enforces the `mustChangePassword` barrier (`:57-65`), checks role membership (`:68-78`), applies the district lifecycle gate including `accessEligible` and the `SUSPENDED`/`CANCELLED`/`SETUP_INCOMPLETE` statuses (`:81-140`), and enforces strict tenant matching (`:143-154`), before parsing the validated actor against `ActorContextSchema` and failing loudly on a corrupt row (`:162-178`). The matrix shows 78 of 87 routes behind it; the nine exceptions are the four `/auth/*` routes, the three public health probes and the two secret-guarded webhooks. Guard attachment was confirmed as `scope.addHook('preHandler', …)` **calls** — not merely imports — in 11 files, each inside the guarded `server.register(async (scope) => …)` body with the `scope.*` registrations after the hooks.
- **No security-relevant plugin is registered globally that should be scoped, and none is scoped that should be global.** `@fastify/cookie`, `@fastify/compress` and `@fastify/cors` are all registered on the root before any route plugin (`entrypoints/http.ts:124`, `:127`, `:136`), which is the correct scope for all three. The one global registration worth naming is `@fastify/compress` with `threshold: 1024` (`:127-130`); no response body carries a CSRF token, a session token or another per-request secret alongside attacker-controlled input, so the BREACH precondition is absent.
- **`Origin` fail-closed behaviour is correct.** `apps/backend/src/modules/auth/origin-guard.ts:8` rejects a missing `Origin`, and `:90-99` rejects a state-changing request carrying neither `Origin` nor a conclusive `Sec-Fetch-Site`. The webhook exemption at `:47-50` is a deliberate path-prefix carve-out, not a gap, because both webhook routes verify the HMAC-derived secret before any handler runs.

##### Reconciliation against the original auditor report

This phase was audited and then independently verified. The verifier's verdicts changed four findings and refuted a fifth. Every proposed ID is accounted for:

- **H-23 — RECORDED, corrected.** The auditor's version claimed `headersTimeout` was left untouched by Fastify. The verifier showed `headersTimeout` is **not a Fastify option at all** — it is absent from `defaultInitOptions` — so Node's own 60 s default applies and there is no Fastify-imposed defect on that bound. The `headersTimeout` claim is dropped. In exchange the verifier found a **third** disabled bound the auditor missed: `handlerTimeout: 0`, also assigned from `defaultInitOptions` and therefore no handler-duration ceiling. The finding was already High and stays High.
- **M-43 — RECORDED as proposed.** The verifier confirmed all five links of the chain (`http.ts:100` → `auth-routes.ts:35-42` → `rate-limiter.ts:14-18` → `Caddyfile:19` → `docker-compose.prod.yml:20-53`) and agreed with the "latent, not live" conclusion. No change.
- **M-44 — RECORDED as proposed.** The verifier confirmed the mechanism at `origin-guard.ts:23-25` and `:28-32` and agreed the exploit is purely theoretical, for the reason the auditor gave (a browser cannot make `Origin` and `Host` disagree in the attacker's favour). No change.
- **M-45 — RECORDED as proposed.** Confirmed as a new finding; the backlog search for `response schema|serialization|allowlist` returned no prior recording of the absence of response serialization as a class.
- **M-46 — WITHDRAWN as a duplicate; the number is reused for the CSP finding.** The auditor's proposed M-46 (error-envelope pass-through) is a re-discovery of two already-recorded findings. Its three sub-claims are exactly **F-8** (backlog L283: `details`/`blockers` forwarded with no `statusCode < 500` guard, `api-error-envelope.ts:176-179` and `:171-174`) plus **F-15** (backlog L299: `resolveErrorCode` returns any non-empty `error.code` verbatim, `:122-128`). The only sub-claim not previously recorded is the 4xx raw-message forwarding at `:130-136` — which is the deliberate carve-out the code comments about, and which is now recorded as the rewritten **L-32** instead. **The duplicate must not be added; the freed number carries the CSP finding.**
- **Proposed M-47 — RECORDED, renumbered to M-46.** No CSP finding was recorded anywhere in the backlog (`CSP|Content-Security|helmet|security header|X-Frame` returns only unrelated `CSPRNG` substring matches). It is a genuinely new finding and takes the freed M-46 slot.
- **Proposed L-32 — REFUTED and REWRITTEN.** The auditor claimed a malformed JSON body yields `500 INTERNAL_ERROR`. `content-type-parser.js:289-290` assigns `statusCode = 400` to the bare `SyntaxError` before it reaches `setErrorHandler`, so `resolveStatusCode` returns 400 and `resolveErrorMessage` takes the `< 500` branch. The real defect is the **inverse**: a 4xx path forwards the raw V8 parser message to the client. The rewritten finding occupies the same L-32 slot.
- **Proposed L-33 — RECORDED.** The prototype-poisoning-guard displacement is confirmed as code, and the override mechanism was additionally verified by the orchestrator against `content-type-parser.js:105-117`; see the finding's Confidence line.
- **Proposed L-34 — MERGED, not recorded as a new finding.** The verifier judged it a partial overlap with **H-17**, whose evidence sentence already states the global fact (backlog L891: "Only rate limiter in the codebase is the login limiter"). Worded as "the only rate limiter is sign-in", it is H-17's evidence restated. The distinct part — the authenticated, non-ingestion surface — is folded into the L-34 note below rather than given an ID.
- **Proposed L-35 — RECORDED as L-34, narrowed.** (The proposal was drafted against the pre-compaction numbering; the Low-series compaction below renumbered it to L-34.) The verifier tied the default-password half to **F-12** (backlog L293). Only the published host port and the previously unrecorded root-level `docker-compose.yml` copy are carried.
- **Proposed L-36 — WITHDRAWN.** The auditor's origin-guard availability item (state-changing routes unreachable to a client sending neither `Origin` nor `Sec-Fetch-Site`) describes the same fail-closed tail already recorded as verified sound at backlog L123 (`origin-guard.ts:8`, `:90-99`). The auditor itself called the behaviour "the correct fail-closed choice"; it is an availability note, not a defect, and is folded into the L-34 note below.
- **Proposed L-37 — WITHDRAWN.** The auditor's health-version item (`/api/v1/health` reports a hardcoded version) is factually correct — `health-routes.ts:231` and `:238` both emit `version: '1.0.0'` while `apps/backend/package.json:3` declares `"version": "0.1.0"` — but it is a cosmetic correctness defect on an informational field, disclosed deliberately by a public probe. It does not meet this backlog's Low bar. The fact is recorded in the "Not determined" notes below so it is not lost, and no ID is consumed.
- **OQ-48 — RECORDED as proposed.** New open question; no collision (the only pre-existing occurrence in the backlog is the reservation line at L3).

**Low-series compaction (recorded deliberately).** Because nothing from this phase had ever been written to disk, no committed identifier was being renumbered. The two withdrawn Lows above (the origin-guard availability item and the health-version item) were therefore removed from the sequence and the remaining Lows were renumbered **contiguously from L-32** — giving **L-32, L-33, L-34 with no gap**. The alternative (leaving two permanent holes at the end of the Low series) was rejected: a hole records an ID that never existed in any committed revision, which is more misleading than a compacted series. The next free Low ID is therefore **L-35**.

##### Routed open questions

**OQ-39 — RESOLVED for the repo-declared edge; STILL OPEN for anything outside the repo.** Within the repo the answer is no: `deploy/compose/Caddyfile` contains no rate-limiting directive of any kind (all 45 lines read), and the image that loads it is stock — `Dockerfile:48` is `FROM caddy:2-alpine` with `COPY deploy/compose/Caddyfile /etc/caddy/Caddyfile` at `:50` and no `xcaddy` build step, so the third-party `rate_limit` Caddy module is not compiled in and could not be configured even if a directive were present. `deploy/compose/docker-compose.prod.yml:110-127` publishes only 80 and 443 from the `caddy` service. Whether a WAF, a cloud load balancer or a host firewall rule sits in front of that on the actual VPS remains **STILL OPEN** — the missing evidence is VPS/network configuration outside the repository, the same access blocker as OQ-3 and OQ-6. This is consistent with H-17's existing note and does not change H-17's severity.

**OQ-8 — RESOLVED.** The question was whether `sanitizeDriverError` should be applied in the global error handler. For the field that matters it need not be: `resolveErrorMessage` (`api-error-envelope.ts:130-136`) returns a fixed non-diagnostic string for **every** status `>= 500`, unconditionally, so raw driver text is already unreachable regardless of what the driver error says. The residual is not the message but `resolveErrorCode` (`:122-128`), which forwards `error.code` verbatim on every status with no 5xx carve-out — a `pg` error's `23505` or `42P01` is relayed to the client as the envelope `code`. So no sanitizer is required for message text; the `code` field is the gap, and it is already recorded as **F-15** (backlog L299). OQ-8's reference to "F-8/F-15" is satisfied by those two existing entries; this phase allocates no F ID.

**Backlog pointer L1170 — RESOLVED.** Confirmed on disk: `apps/backend/src/adapters/db/client.ts:99-106` does set `error`, and no caller reads it. **No route serialises that field.** The verifier corrected the auditor's *method* here: the auditor said only `health-routes.ts` calls `checkDbHealth`, but there are **two production call sites plus one more** — `health-routes.ts:176`, `health-routes.ts:223`, and `apps/backend/src/modules/health/health-checker.ts:132` (inside `checkDatabaseHealth`). The conclusion is unchanged and in fact strengthened: `health-routes.ts:195-203` and `:228-239` send only literal enums plus a timestamp, and `health-checker.ts` builds its own hardcoded message rather than reading `probe.error`.

**Backlog pointer L1171 — RESOLVED.** Confirmed against the pinned source: `error-handler.js:48-49` installs the prototype-chain successor as the next handler *before* invoking the custom one, and `:63-75` catches any throw and calls `reply.send(err)`, which re-enters `handleError` against `rootErrorHandler` and terminates in `defaultErrorHandler` (`:78-83`) writing a response. No hang, no double-error. Independently, reading both `parse` call sites (`api-error-envelope.ts:143`, `:186`) shows the inputs are constructed so the parse cannot fail. The mechanism is safe for two independent reasons.

**OQ-2 — STILL OPEN (unchanged).** Effective production values of `APP_ORIGIN` and `SESSION_COOKIE_NAME` are not derivable from the repository. What this phase adds: if `APP_ORIGIN` were unset in production, the failure mode is *not* a permissive CORS policy — `entrypoints/http.ts:134` falls back to `http://localhost:5173`, which rejects the real SPA origin, and `origin-guard.ts:8` fails closed on a missing `Origin`. The unset case is a broken application, not an open one. The missing evidence remains the production `.env`.

**OQ-3 — STILL OPEN (narrowed).** From the repository side the declared ingress surface is now confirmed: `deploy/compose/docker-compose.prod.yml:117-119` publishes only `80:80` and `443:443` on the `caddy` service; the `backend` (`:20`), `worker` (`:55`), `userbot` (`:85`) and `postgres` (`:2`) services declare no `ports:` at all; and `Caddyfile:38-44` binds exactly the two canonical hostnames plus the plain-IP redirect. Whether an nginx or firewall layer exists *in front of* Caddy on the host is still not determinable from the repo. **(OQ-48, raised below, is the sharper version of this question.)**

##### Not determined

- **The actual propagation timing of the root-level `addHook`.** The matrix's "Origin guard" column for 11 files rests on the inherited root hook. The auditor read `node_modules/.pnpm/fastify@5.12.0/node_modules/fastify/fastify.js:577-621`, where `addHook` defers via `this.after(...)` and then walks `this[kChildren]` (`:618-621`), which means a child registered *after* that deferred callback runs would not inherit it — the exact hazard backlog M-5 describes and Phase 1.2 resolved in favour of app-wide application (backlog L123, L135). This phase used Phase 1.2's established conclusion rather than re-deriving it, because re-deriving it requires either executing the server (barred) or reading avvio's queueing in a second pinned dependency. **If Phase 1.2's conclusion is ever retracted, every `R` cell in the matrix must be re-read as possibly absent.**
- **The response object shape for 82 of 87 routes.** M-45 confirms structurally that no output filtering exists and read two handlers (`audit-routes.ts:48-53`, `:79-94`) to confirm they pass service-layer values straight to `reply.send`. The other 80 handlers and their services were not read, so M-45 asserts the *absence of a control*, not any specific field leak.
- **`@fastify/compress` behaviour on the error path and its `Vary` header.** Only the registration options were read (`entrypoints/http.ts:127-130`); the plugin's internals were not, so it cannot be stated whether compressed error responses emit `Vary: Accept-Encoding` or whether the 1 KB threshold is applied before or after serialization. The BREACH precondition is absent on the available evidence (no per-request secret in any response body), so this changes no severity.
- **Whether `resolveErrorCode`'s pass-through is reachable with a sensitive code today.** The mechanism is confirmed (`api-error-envelope.ts:122-128`) and `pg` errors lack `statusCode` and therefore take the 5xx branch for their *message*. The `code` values of every error type thrown across the 87 handlers were not enumerated, so F-15's blast radius is bounded by inspection of the envelope, not by a census of callers.
- **`errorDetail` content in `health-checker.ts`.** `assertPrivacyBoundary` exists at `apps/backend/src/modules/health/health-checker.ts:37-47` and does token/auth-URL pattern redaction (`:43-44`), and health observations *are* serialized into responses via the `response:` schemas at `health-routes.ts:256` and `:280`. What `errorDetail` is derived from, and whether the sanitizer's coverage at `:41-45` is complete for it, was not read. Flagged rather than guessed; it does not affect pointer L1170, where `probe.error` is definitively never read by any caller.
- **HEAD route surface.** `exposeHeadRoutes` defaults to `true` (`config-validator.js:1265`), so each of the 40 GET registrations also answers HEAD. Whether a HEAD response inherits the same guards was not tested — it should, since the guards are `preHandler` hooks — and no claim is made either way.
- **Caddy's own default timeouts and TLS minimum version.** `deploy/compose/Caddyfile` has no `servers { timeouts { … } }` and no `tls { … }` block, so both fall to Caddy 2's built-in defaults. The `caddy:2-alpine` image's default global options were not read, so H-23 asserts only that *no* timeout is configured in this repository, not what Caddy's fallback happens to be. Neither compose file declares `ulimits:`, so the host file-descriptor ceiling that bounds the attack is also unquantified.
- **Informational fact recorded here because no ID was allocated for it:** the public `GET /api/v1/health` response embeds `version: '1.0.0'` as a literal at `apps/backend/src/modules/health/health-routes.ts:231` and `:238`, while `apps/backend/package.json:3` declares `"version": "0.1.0"`. The field is unauthenticated, so it is disclosed to any caller, and it is wrong today. An operator or probe that trusts it to decide which revision is live will be misled. Cosmetic; not scored.
- **Live state of any kind:** database contents and `pgboss` rows, the production `.env`, the VPS filesystem, and the deployed process topology. None were accessed; all are out of scope for this phase.

##### Suggested pointers

Five pointers follow from this phase. Following the convention every other phase uses, they are carried once in the **Pointers raised by Phase 4.1, routed to their owning layers** list under `## Cross-Layer Pointers` at the end of this document, and are not repeated here.

#### Phase 4.2 — Container & Deploy Correctness

Phase verdict: The container topology is largely sound — the multi-stage build is layered correctly, the runtime image drops to a non-root user, no secret enters the build context, the production compose fails fast on a missing database password, and PostgreSQL publishes no host port in the production stack. The defects are at the **deploy transition** and the **operator surface**. Two Highs: the repository has no production migration step at all (schema DDL is applied by test files and CI, never by the deploy path), and no service in the production compose declares an `image:` key, so the runbook's documented `docker compose pull && docker compose up -d` deploy **cannot ever apply new code**. The Mediums are a bind-mount whose host source does not exist and whose in-image preparation is masked, a PostgreSQL major-version drift between production and every other environment, and an env template whose AI provider is not a value any code path recognises. This phase also answers three of the four routed Layer 4 pointers and finds two further deploy-surface defects that no prior phase had recorded.

**Counts: 0 Critical, 2 High, 3 Medium, 3 Low.**

**Method.** Read-only. The deploy surface was enumerated from the filesystem rather than assumed, then every artifact that could affect a deployment was read in full: the root `Dockerfile`, all three compose files, the Caddyfile, the env example, `deploy/README.md`, the userbot runbook, `.dockerignore`, the CI workflow, the workspace and backend manifests, and the application files that the deploy path actually invokes (`http.ts`, `worker.ts`, `migrate.ts`, `boss-client.ts`, `external-tombstone-store.ts`, `system-backup-verifier.ts`). No build, no install, no typecheck, no test, no network call, no SSH and no database command was run. **Every path and line number below was derived from the files on disk at the time of writing**; no path or line was copied from prose. Where a conclusion needs the production host it is recorded as an OQ, not as a finding.

---

##### High

**H-24. No migration step exists on the production deploy path — schema DDL is applied only by test suites and CI**

- Severity: High
- Evidence (OBSERVED): `C:\codevision-works\mahalla-ovozi\Dockerfile:45` — the backend container's entrypoint is `CMD ["pnpm", "--filter", "@mahalla-ovozi/backend", "exec", "node", "--import", "tsx/esm", "src/entrypoints/http.ts"]`. That entrypoint never migrates: `C:\codevision-works\mahalla-ovozi\apps\backend\src\entrypoints\http.ts:93-101` builds the Fastify instance and `:179-181` calls `boss.start()` then `initBossQueues(boss)` — pg-boss queues are bootstrapped, application schema is not. The migration entrypoint `C:\codevision-works\mahalla-ovozi\apps\backend\src\adapters\db\migrate.ts:13` exports `runMigrations`, and `:35-40` makes it a CLI when invoked directly — but a repository-wide search for `runMigrations` outside `node_modules` returns **only** its own definition at `migrate.ts:13,36` and 14 importers, every one of which is a file under `C:\codevision-works\mahalla-ovozi\apps\backend\tests\` (e.g. `tests/district-backup-expiry.test.ts:26`, `tests/disaster-restore-reconciliation.test.ts:42`, `tests/district-live-deletion.test.ts:49`). The `pnpm db:migrate` script (`C:\codevision-works\mahalla-ovozi\apps\backend\package.json:12`) appears outside the backlog in exactly one place: `C:\codevision-works\mahalla-ovozi\.github\workflows\ci.yml:53` — `run: pnpm db:migrate`, against the CI service container. `C:\codevision-works\mahalla-ovozi\deploy\compose\docker-compose.prod.yml` declares five services (`postgres` `:2`, `backend` `:20`, `worker` `:55`, `userbot` `:85`, `caddy` `:110`) and **no production service runs a migration step** — none of the five invokes `runMigrations`, and there is no init container and no one-shot migrate service. (Test suites and CI do run migrations; the gap is specific to the production deploy path.) The documented deploy is `C:\codevision-works\mahalla-ovozi\deploy\README.md:58-62` — `docker compose pull && docker compose up -d` — with no migrate step before or after.
- Mechanism: In CI the schema is created as a side effect of running the test suite, so the repository's own feedback loop never exercises the "apply migrations to a production database" path. The production topology has no component whose job is to run `runMigrations`, and the process that does boot (`http.ts`) connects to whatever schema it finds. The migration SQL is present — `migrate.ts:17` resolves `path.resolve(__dirname, '../../../drizzle')`, which is `apps/backend/drizzle`, and that directory exists — but nothing in the deploy surface executes it.
- Failure scenario: A fresh production host (new VPS, rebuilt volume, disaster restore to a clean instance) comes up healthy by every declared signal: `postgres` passes its `pg_isready` healthcheck (`docker-compose.prod.yml:12-16`), `backend` passes its `curl http://localhost:3000/api/v1/health/live` healthcheck (`:49`) because the liveness probe does not touch application tables, and `caddy` starts because `backend` is healthy (`:123-125`). The stack then serves 500s on every query against a missing table. The failure is invisible to the declared health semantics and presents as an application error, not a deploy error. The same gap applies to any future schema change: the migration file lands, the image is rebuilt, and the column does not exist.
- Proposed fix: Add an explicit migration step to the deploy path. The narrowest correct change is a dedicated one-shot service in `deploy/compose/docker-compose.prod.yml` built from the same `runner` target that runs `pnpm --filter @mahalla-ovozi/backend db:migrate` and exits, with `backend`, `worker` and `userbot` declaring `depends_on: {migrate: {condition: service_completed_successfully}}` in place of their current `service_healthy` dependency on `backend`. `runMigrations` already takes a database-level advisory lock (`migrate.ts:11,20,26`, `MIGRATION_ADVISORY_LOCK_ID = 847291047129481`), so a repeated or concurrent invocation is safe and this service is idempotent. Regardless of the mechanism, `deploy/README.md` must gain the migrate step so the runbook stops describing an incomplete deploy.
- Confidence: Confirmed for the repository (no migrate invocation exists on any deploy path; verified by exhaustive search outside `node_modules`). The production database's current schema state cannot be read from here — see the reconciliation note on OQ-46 and the OQ-47 family.

**H-25. No service in the production compose declares an `image:` key, so the documented deploy command can never apply new code**

- Severity: High
- Evidence (OBSERVED): In `C:\codevision-works\mahalla-ovozi\deploy\compose\docker-compose.prod.yml` the only `image:` key in the entire file is `:3` — `image: postgres:16-alpine`.
